What brings me in is that the moment a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable, my heart just starts pounding out of my chest. My hands go completely damp on the steering wheel, or the armrest if my daughter is driving. I feel this terrible, overwhelming rush of fear before I'm even on the bridge. It's awful. You know, my daughter, she drives a Subaru now. Used to have a Honda, but she traded it in last year. Got a good deal on it, too, the dealership over on Route 9. The salesman was a nice young man, reminded me of my nephew, Thomas. Thomas just graduated with his master's in engineering, if you can believe it. We had a lovely dinner for him at that Italian place downtown, the one with the good garlic bread... but anyway, yes, the bridges. It's just a terrible feeling.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that trigger this fear, or are there other situations that cause you a similar level of anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, it's... it's really just the bridges. I don't have this kind of trouble anywhere else. I mean, I go all over town just fine. Like the other day, I went down to the hardware store on Main Street to get some of those little felt pads for the bottom of my dining room chairs. You know, the hardwood floors get scratched so easily if you aren't careful. We had them refinished back in... oh, it must have been 1998. The fellow who did it, his name was Arthur. Or maybe Artie? He had this beautiful old golden retriever that used to just sit in the back of his truck while he worked. I've always loved dogs. But at my age—I'm 87 now—it's just too much work to walk a dog in the winter. The ice on the sidewalks is terrible around my neighborhood, the city never salts them properly, and you can slip before you even know what happened. But, uh... what was it you were asking me? Oh, right, other situations. No, no, just the bridges. Nothing else makes my heart pound like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself going out of your way to avoid bridges when you can?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. For more than two years now, I’ve been planning every single one of my errands and visits just to avoid them. I’ve completely given up my trips to the San Juan islands, and my theater nights down in Seattle. Oh, the theater in Seattle used to be just marvelous. My late wife and I used to go see the musicals all the time. She adored Rodgers and Hammerstein. We saw *South Pacific* there, oh, it must have been the late 70s? The costumes were so vibrant. I think I still have the playbill in a box up in the attic. It gets terribly dusty up there, though. I really ought to hire the neighbor boy, Billy, to clean it out for me. He mows my lawn on Tuesdays. Good kid, saving up for college. He wants to study agriculture, I think, maybe over at Washington State... but anyway, what was I saying? \n\nOh, right, avoiding the bridges. Yes, I just don't go to Seattle anymore. And if I absolutely *must* cross one, well, it's a whole production. I have to study the route ahead of time, and the timing has to be just so to avoid traffic. Traffic is dreadful lately anyway, people are always looking at their phones. Just last week I saw a man eating a bowl of soup while driving! Can you imagine? Soup! Looked like minestrone. My mother made a wonderful minestrone, she used to put a parmesan rind in the broth to give it flavor. That's the secret, you know. \n\nBut yes, if I have to cross a bridge, I need someone in the car with me, usually my daughter, and I just sit there completely frightened the whole way across.\n\nDoctor: Are there other activities or things you've stopped doing because of your fear of bridges?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. I've had to give up a good deal, actually. Like I mentioned, the trips to the islands and the theater outings are completely gone. But I also used to volunteer at the historical society on Thursdays, sorting through the old photographs. Fascinating stuff, really. You'd see pictures of the town from back in the 1920s, when Main Street was still dirt. There was this one photo of the old general store, the one that burned down in '45. My uncle Henry used to buy his chewing tobacco there. He chewed Red Man, always had a tin in his breast pocket. He was a character, Henry was. Used to play the fiddle at the barn dances out in the valley. They don't have barn dances like that anymore, do they? Now it's all... well, I don't even know what the kids listen to these days. Electronic noises, mostly. \n\nBut anyway, yes, I had to stop the volunteering because the quickest way there was over the suspension bridge, and I just couldn't stomach it anymore. So my days have become quite a bit smaller, I suppose you could say. I just stay closer to home. And I'm relying on my daughter and others for plans I used to handle all by myself. It's frustrating, being 87 and feeling like you can't even drive yourself to sort some old pictures. But that bridge... it's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of bridges affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it's ruined any relationships, no. My daughter is very patient with me, bless her heart. She takes me where I need to go if it involves a bridge. She’s always been a helpful girl. I remember when she was in the Girl Scouts—oh, she must have been about ten or eleven—she sold the most cookies in her entire troop. We had boxes of Thin Mints stacked all the way up to the ceiling in the garage! I used to sneak a sleeve of them into the freezer. Have you ever had a frozen Thin Mint? Oh, they are just spectacular with a cup of black coffee in the afternoon. I still use the percolator my wife bought us in 1968. Makes a much better cup than those little plastic pods everyone uses now. The coffee just tastes... I don't know, richer, somehow. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my friends and family. No, they understand, mostly. I mean, I don't see the folks from the historical society as much, but my daughter and the neighbors, they're fine about it. They just know I don't do bridges anymore.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the impact of your fear of bridges on your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's... it's just made my world a bit smaller, you know? I feel a bit more confined. Not that I don't like my home, I love my home. My wife and I bought this house in 1962, right after the Cuban Missile Crisis. I remember we were so worried about the state of the world, but we wanted a place with a nice yard. And we got one! I planted a dogwood tree in the front that very first spring. It blooms these beautiful white flowers every April. The petals look like little crosses, if you look closely. The neighbor's cat, Whiskers, likes to sleep under it in the summer. He’s an orange tabby, quite fat, really. I think Mrs. Higgins feeds him too much tuna. I told her, \"Mildred, you're going to give that cat gout,\" but she just laughed. She makes a lovely rhubarb pie, though. Brings me a slice every Sunday. Rhubarb is tricky to grow, you know, you have to make sure the soil is just right... \n\nBut, well, I'm not really sure how else to describe the impact, honestly. It's just a nuisance, having to avoid the bridges, but I manage well enough here at the house.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically about the bridge itself, or does the thought of traveling to places that might require crossing a bridge also cause you anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, it's really the bridge itself, I suppose. I mean, I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without a problem. Logically, I know they're built to hold all that weight. They use reinforced steel and concrete, you know. My brother-in-law, Arthur, he used to work in construction. Not bridges, mostly commercial buildings downtown. He helped pour the foundation for the old bank building on 4th Street. He always said concrete is only as good as the rebar you put in it. Arthur was a smart man, could fix any engine you put in front of him. He had this old Ford pickup, a '54, I think. Painted it cherry red himself in his driveway. The smell of that paint lingered for weeks. His wife, my sister Margaret, she wasn't too happy about that. She was very particular about smells, Margaret was. Couldn't stand the smell of garlic, either. Made Italian dinners very difficult at her house... \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, the bridges. Even knowing they're safe, when I just see a high span on a map, or if I spot one ahead through the windshield... my heart just drops. It feels to me as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away right under us. So, yes, just the thought of the bridge itself is what really gets me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges worse when the bridge is particularly high or over water?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the height is the worst part. If it's one of those little stone bridges over a creek, I don't mind so much. But those big ones, the ones that go way up into the sky... oh, I just can't. And over water, yes, that's terrible too. Especially the ocean or a big river. The Mississippi River is quite a sight, isn't it? My wife and I took a riverboat cruise down the Mississippi back in '85. Or was it '86? No, it must have been '85 because that was the year the Chicago Bears won the Super Bowl. What a team they had that year. Walter Payton, they called him \"Sweetness.\" He ran like a gazelle, just beautiful to watch. I used to play football in high school, you know. I was a halfback. We didn't have the fancy helmets they have now, just leather caps. I broke my nose twice! My mother was furious. She said I was going to ruin my handsome face. She was a very strict woman, my mother. She used to make us eat liver and onions every Tuesday... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the high bridges and the ones over the water are definitely the ones that terrify me the most. I just can't go near them.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges the same regardless of whether you are driving, walking, or a passenger?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I haven't really noticed if it makes a difference. To be honest, I haven't tried walking across one in, oh, it must be decades. I remember walking across the Golden Gate Bridge once, a long time ago. My wife and I went to San Francisco for our tenth anniversary. We took one of those cable cars, you know, the ones that go up and down the steep hills. The conductor was a fellow named Giuseppe, had a mustache you wouldn't believe, curled right up at the ends. He told us he used to be a baker in North Beach. Said the secret to a good sourdough is the starter, you have to feed it every day like a pet. We bought a loaf from a bakery down by the wharf, ate it right out of the paper bag while we watched the sea lions. They make such a racket, those sea lions! Barking and carrying on, fighting over space on the wooden docks. Reminded me of my nephews, Tommy and Billy, always wrestling in the living room... \n\nBut, no, I don't really know if it's worse driving or being a passenger. I just know I don't want to be on one at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges is partly because of how high they are, or is it something else about the bridges themselves that scares you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really don't know for sure. I mean, we talked about the height earlier, and yes, that's dreadful. But is it just the height? Or something else? I couldn't say exactly. It's just the whole... the whole structure of it, I suppose. \n\nYou know, speaking of structures, my grandfather used to build these incredible birdhouses. Not just little boxes, mind you. They were like miniature mansions. He built one that looked exactly like a Victorian home, complete with tiny little shingles he cut from cedar scraps. He'd spend hours in his workshop out back. It smelled like sawdust and pipe tobacco in there. He smoked Cherry Blend, came in a red and white pouch. My grandmother hated it, said it made the curtains smell. She was always washing the curtains, it seemed. Had this old wringer washer on the back porch. You had to be so careful feeding the clothes through those rollers, or you'd pinch your fingers something awful. My cousin Sally got her hair caught in one once! Oh, what a commotion that was. We had to cut a big chunk of her hair right off. She cried for three days straight, wouldn't even come out of her room for Sunday dinner. And my grandmother made the best pot roast for Sunday dinner, with carrots and potatoes roasted right in the juices...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, what was it you were asking? If it's the height or something else? I really just don't know. It's just the bridges themselves, the whole idea of them, that I can't stand.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar kind of fear when you are in other high places, like tall buildings or mountains?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not the height itself, really. I mean, I can stand in a tall building just fine. I remember when they finished the Sears Tower in Chicago, oh, what a marvel that was. I took my son up to the observation deck when he was just a little boy. We stood right against the glass and looked out over the whole city. You could see all the way to Indiana on a clear day. He had this little toy airplane he carried everywhere, a die-cast metal P-51 Mustang. He kept zooming it against the window, pretending he was flying over the buildings. He wanted to be a pilot for the longest time, right up until he realized he needed glasses. Poor kid was devastated. We ended up getting him these thick, black-rimmed glasses, just like Buddy Holly used to wear. You remember Buddy Holly? \"Peggy Sue,\" \"That'll Be the Day\"... such a tragedy, that plane crash. The day the music died, they called it. Don McLean wrote a whole song about it, a very long song. I heard it on the radio just the other day while I was trying to tune in the baseball game...\n\nBut, no, buildings or mountains don't bother me. It's just the bridges. I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without a problem. It's perfectly safe, logically. But when I see a high span on the map, or if I see one looming ahead through the windshield... oh, it just feels to me as if the whole thing is waiting to drop away right out from under us. Just collapse completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience anxiety or fear when in other high places, such as standing near a cliff or at the top of a ladder?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I don't really know for sure. Ladders, you say? I haven't been up on a ladder in years. My son comes over to clean the gutters now. He's got one of those fancy aluminum extension ladders, very lightweight. Not like the old wooden one I used to have. That thing weighed a ton. I bought it at a hardware store downtown, back when downtown was still bustling. There was a soda fountain right next door, Woolworth's, I think it was. They made the best cherry phosphates. You don't see cherry phosphates anymore. It's all these energy drinks now, in those brightly colored cans. My grandson drinks them like water. I tell him all that sugar and caffeine can't be good for his heart, but you know teenagers. They think they're invincible. He's playing football this year, defensive tackle. He's a big boy, gets it from his mother's side... \n\nBut, what were you asking? Cliffs and ladders? I don't think I've really noticed if they bother me. It's just the bridges that get me so worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when standing on a balcony of a high-rise building?\n\nPatient: Balconies? Oh, I haven't been out on a balcony in... well, I couldn't tell you the last time. My wife and I, we stayed in a lovely hotel in Miami once, must have been back in the late seventies. Or was it the early eighties? No, it was '79, because that was the year they had that terrible winter up north, snowed all through February. We had a room right on the ocean, with a little wrought-iron balcony. Beautiful scrolling work on it, painted white. We'd sit out there in the mornings and watch the seagulls. They were so bold, those gulls. One of them swooped right down and stole a piece of toast right off my plate! Can you believe it? Just snatched it and flew off. We laughed about that for years. We used to have toast with orange marmalade. You just don't find good marmalade these days, not like they used to make. Now it's all just sugar jelly with a few peels thrown in. My mother used to make her own preserves, apricot and plum. She'd spend the whole end of summer canning in the kitchen. It was so hot in there, but she'd just tie an apron on and get to work... \n\nBut, I'm sorry, you were asking about standing on balconies? I really haven't had the occasion to try it lately, so I just don't know if it would bother me. It's really only those bridges that give me that dreadful feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar kind of fear when you're in other situations that might make you feel like you're in a high place, but not necessarily on a bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really don't think so. Other high places, you mean? Like an airplane or something? We flew out to California back in '92 for my niece's wedding. She married a lovely boy, an architect. They designed their own house out there in Pasadena, beautiful place with these big exposed wooden beams. Cedar, I think they were. Smelled wonderful when you walked in. They served prime rib at the reception, but it was a bit too rare for my taste. I always liked my meat cooked through. My father was a butcher, you know. Had his own shop on 4th Street. He could carve a roast like nobody's business. Used this big, heavy cleaver with a wooden handle that was worn smooth from years of use. He'd sharpen it every morning on a whetstone. The sound of that stone scraping against the steel, shhhk, shhhk, shhhk... it's funny the things you remember. He used to slip me a piece of bologna when I'd come in after school... \n\nBut, no, I don't think I have that same feeling in other high places. It really is just the bridges. Just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when on a bridge, compared to when you're on another type of elevated structure, like an overpass?\n\nPatient: Well, an overpass... I suppose I haven't really thought about the difference. An overpass is just a road over another road, isn't it? We used to drive over the one on I-95 all the time when we'd go visit my sister in Connecticut. She lived in this charming little colonial house in Milford. Had a big oak tree in the front yard that must have been a hundred years old. In the autumn, the leaves would turn this brilliant shade of orange, and we'd spend all afternoon raking them into massive piles. The kids would just dive right into them, laughing and getting leaves all in their hair. My sister had a golden retriever back then, Barnaby. Oh, he was a good dog. He'd jump in the leaves right along with the kids. He had this funny habit of carrying a tennis ball around in his mouth everywhere he went. Even when he was sleeping, he'd have that ball tucked right under his chin. Poor old Barnaby, he got terrible arthritis in his back legs toward the end... \n\nBut an overpass, you were asking? No, I don't think overpasses bother me. It's just the bridges over water, mostly. Just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges limited to certain types, such as suspension bridges or those with a certain design?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't really know if it's the design. Suspension bridges, you mean? Like the Golden Gate? We saw that when we went to California, too. We didn't drive over it, though, thank goodness. We took a ferry across the bay instead. Oh, the ferry was wonderful! The wind blowing in your hair, the smell of the salt water. They sold these little cups of clam chowder on board. It wasn't as good as New England clam chowder, of course. My wife made the best chowder. She'd use fresh clams we'd dig up ourselves when we vacationed in Cape Cod. You have to soak them in saltwater first, you know, with a little cornmeal, so they spit out the sand. If you don't do that, your soup is all gritty. Nobody likes gritty soup. She'd serve it with those little oyster crackers... the ones shaped like hexagons. I used to buy them in big tins at the grocer... \n\nBut, I'm sorry, you were asking about the types of bridges? I really couldn't tell you if it's the suspension ones or the arches or what. I just know that when I see a bridge, especially a big one over the water, my heart just starts pounding. I don't think I've ever really paid attention to the design, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges might be connected to a fear of being in high places in general?\n\nPatient: Well, high places in general... I don't really think so. I mean, we used to live on the fourteenth floor of this apartment building back in the city. It was a lovely place, right near the park. You could look out the window and see all the trees changing colors in the fall. We had this little balcony, too. My wife would grow tomatoes out there in these big terracotta pots. She had quite the green thumb, she did. She'd talk to the plants, you know, said it helped them grow. I thought it was silly, but those tomatoes were the sweetest things you ever tasted. We'd slice them up thick and put them on a sandwich with a little mayonnaise and some fresh black pepper. Oh, and the bread... there was this Italian bakery down on the corner that made the best sourdough. The crust was so thick and chewy, you really had to work at it. The baker, Tony, he'd always give me an extra loaf if I came in right before closing. He was a good man, Tony. Had a son who played baseball for the minor leagues...\n\nBut, no, the fourteenth floor never bothered me. Looking out over the balcony, being up high like that... it was fine. It really is just... just the bridges. I don't know why, but it's just the bridges.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It feels to me as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away right under us. So, yes, just the thought of the bridge itself is what really gets me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges worse when the bridge is particularly high or over water?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the height is the worst part. If it's one of those little stone bridges over a creek, I don't mind so much. But those big ones, the ones that go way up into the sky... oh, I just can't. And over water, yes, that's terrible too. Especially the ocean or a big river. The Mississippi River is quite a sight, isn't it? My wife and I took a riverboat cruise down the Mississippi back in '85. Or was it '86? No, it must have been '85 because that was the year the Chicago Bears won the Super Bowl. What a team they had that year. Walter Payton, they called him \"Sweetness.\" He ran like a gazelle, just beautiful to watch. I used to play football in high school, you know. I was a halfback. We didn't have the fancy helmets they have now, just leather caps. I broke my nose twice! My mother was furious. She said I was going to ruin my handsome face. She was a very strict woman, my mother. She used to make us eat liver and onions every Tuesday... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the high bridges and the ones over the water are definitely the ones that terrify me the most. I just can't go near them.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges the same regardless of whether you are driving, walking, or a passenger?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I haven't really noticed if it makes a difference. To be honest, I haven't tried walking across one in, oh, it must be decades. I remember walking across the Golden Gate Bridge once, a long time ago. My wife and I went to San Francisco for our tenth anniversary. We took one of those cable cars, you know, the ones that go up and down the steep hills. The conductor was a fellow named Giuseppe, had a mustache you wouldn't believe, curled right up at the ends. He told us he used to be a baker in North Beach. Said the secret to a good sourdough is the starter, you have to feed it every day like a pet. We bought a loaf from a bakery down by the wharf, ate it right out of the paper bag while we watched the sea lions. They make such a racket, those sea lions! Barking and carrying on, fighting over space on the wooden docks. Reminded me of my nephews, Tommy and Billy, always wrestling in the living room... \n\nBut, no, I don't really know if it's worse driving or being a passenger. I just know I don't want to be on one at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges is partly because of how high they are, or is it something else about the bridges themselves that scares you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really don't know for sure. I mean, we talked about the height earlier, and yes, that's dreadful. But is it just the height? Or something else? I couldn't say exactly. It's just the whole... the whole structure of it, I suppose. \n\nYou know, speaking of structures, my grandfather used to build these incredible birdhouses. Not just little boxes, mind you. They were like miniature mansions. He built one that looked exactly like a Victorian home, complete with tiny little shingles he cut from cedar scraps. He'd spend hours in his workshop out back. It smelled like sawdust and pipe tobacco in there. He smoked Cherry Blend, came in a red and white pouch. My grandmother hated it, said it made the curtains smell. She was always washing the curtains, it seemed. Had this old wringer washer on the back porch. You had to be so careful feeding the clothes through those rollers, or you'd pinch your fingers something awful. My cousin Sally got her hair caught in one once! Oh, what a commotion that was. We had to cut a big chunk of her hair right off. She cried for three days straight, wouldn't even come out of her room for Sunday dinner. And my grandmother made the best pot roast for Sunday dinner, with carrots and potatoes roasted right in the juices...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, what was it you were asking? If it's the height or something else? I really just don't know. It's just the bridges themselves, the whole idea of them, that I can't stand.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar kind of fear when you are in other high places, like tall buildings or mountains?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not the height itself, really. I mean, I can stand in a tall building just fine. I remember when they finished the Sears Tower in Chicago, oh, what a marvel that was. I took my son up to the observation deck when he was just a little boy. We stood right against the glass and looked out over the whole city. You could see all the way to Indiana on a clear day. He had this little toy airplane he carried everywhere, a die-cast metal P-51 Mustang. He kept zooming it against the window, pretending he was flying over the buildings. He wanted to be a pilot for the longest time, right up until he realized he needed glasses. Poor kid was devastated. We ended up getting him these thick, black-rimmed glasses, just like Buddy Holly used to wear. You remember Buddy Holly? \"Peggy Sue,\" \"That'll Be the Day\"... such a tragedy, that plane crash. The day the music died, they called it. Don McLean wrote a whole song about it, a very long song. I heard it on the radio just the other day while I was trying to tune in the baseball game...\n\nBut, no, buildings or mountains don't bother me. It's just the bridges. I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without a problem. It's perfectly safe, logically. But when I see a high span on the map, or if I see one looming ahead through the windshield... oh, it just feels to me as if the whole thing is waiting to drop away right out from under us. Just collapse completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience anxiety or fear when in other high places, such as standing near a cliff or at the top of a ladder?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I don't really know for sure. Ladders, you say? I haven't been up on a ladder in years. My son comes over to clean the gutters now. He's got one of those fancy aluminum extension ladders, very lightweight. Not like the old wooden one I used to have. That thing weighed a ton. I bought it at a hardware store downtown, back when downtown was still bustling. There was a soda fountain right next door, Woolworth's, I think it was. They made the best cherry phosphates. You don't see cherry phosphates anymore. It's all these energy drinks now, in those brightly colored cans. My grandson drinks them like water. I tell him all that sugar and caffeine can't be good for his heart, but you know teenagers. They think they're invincible. He's playing football this year, defensive tackle. He's a big boy, gets it from his mother's side... \n\nBut, what were you asking? Cliffs and ladders? I don't think I've really noticed if they bother me. It's just the bridges that get me so worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when standing on a balcony of a high-rise building?\n\nPatient: Balconies? Oh, I haven't been out on a balcony in... well, I couldn't tell you the last time. My wife and I, we stayed in a lovely hotel in Miami once, must have been back in the late seventies. Or was it the early eighties? No, it was '79, because that was the year they had that terrible winter up north, snowed all through February. We had a room right on the ocean, with a little wrought-iron balcony. Beautiful scrolling work on it, painted white. We'd sit out there in the mornings and watch the seagulls. They were so bold, those gulls. One of them swooped right down and stole a piece of toast right off my plate! Can you believe it? Just snatched it and flew off. We laughed about that for years. We used to have toast with orange marmalade. You just don't find good marmalade these days, not like they used to make. Now it's all just sugar jelly with a few peels thrown in. My mother used to make her own preserves, apricot and plum. She'd spend the whole end of summer canning in the kitchen. It was so hot in there, but she'd just tie an apron on and get to work... \n\nBut, I'm sorry, you were asking about standing on balconies? I really haven't had the occasion to try it lately, so I just don't know if it would bother me. It's really only those bridges that give me that dreadful feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar kind of fear when you're in other situations that might make you feel like you're in a high place, but not necessarily on a bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really don't think so. Other high places, you mean? Like an airplane or something? We flew out to California back in '92 for my niece's wedding. She married a lovely boy, an architect. They designed their own house out there in Pasadena, beautiful place with these big exposed wooden beams. Cedar, I think they were. Smelled wonderful when you walked in. They served prime rib at the reception, but it was a bit too rare for my taste. I always liked my meat cooked through. My father was a butcher, you know. Had his own shop on 4th Street. He could carve a roast like nobody's business. Used this big, heavy cleaver with a wooden handle that was worn smooth from years of use. He'd sharpen it every morning on a whetstone. The sound of that stone scraping against the steel, shhhk, shhhk, shhhk... it's funny the things you remember. He used to slip me a piece of bologna when I'd come in after school... \n\nBut, no, I don't think I have that same feeling in other high places. It really is just the bridges. Just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when on a bridge, compared to when you're on another type of elevated structure, like an overpass?\n\nPatient: Well, an overpass... I suppose I haven't really thought about the difference. An overpass is just a road over another road, isn't it? We used to drive over the one on I-95 all the time when we'd go visit my sister in Connecticut. She lived in this charming little colonial house in Milford. Had a big oak tree in the front yard that must have been a hundred years old. In the autumn, the leaves would turn this brilliant shade of orange, and we'd spend all afternoon raking them into massive piles. The kids would just dive right into them, laughing and getting leaves all in their hair. My sister had a golden retriever back then, Barnaby. Oh, he was a good dog. He'd jump in the leaves right along with the kids. He had this funny habit of carrying a tennis ball around in his mouth everywhere he went. Even when he was sleeping, he'd have that ball tucked right under his chin. Poor old Barnaby, he got terrible arthritis in his back legs toward the end... \n\nBut an overpass, you were asking? No, I don't think overpasses bother me. It's just the bridges over water, mostly. Just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges limited to certain types, such as suspension bridges or those with a certain design?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't really know if it's the design. Suspension bridges, you mean? Like the Golden Gate? We saw that when we went to California, too. We didn't drive over it, though, thank goodness. We took a ferry across the bay instead. Oh, the ferry was wonderful! The wind blowing in your hair, the smell of the salt water. They sold these little cups of clam chowder on board. It wasn't as good as New England clam chowder, of course. My wife made the best chowder. She'd use fresh clams we'd dig up ourselves when we vacationed in Cape Cod. You have to soak them in saltwater first, you know, with a little cornmeal, so they spit out the sand. If you don't do that, your soup is all gritty. Nobody likes gritty soup. She'd serve it with those little oyster crackers... the ones shaped like hexagons. I used to buy them in big tins at the grocer... \n\nBut, I'm sorry, you were asking about the types of bridges? I really couldn't tell you if it's the suspension ones or the arches or what. I just know that when I see a bridge, especially a big one over the water, my heart just starts pounding. I don't think I've ever really paid attention to the design, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges might be connected to a fear of being in high places in general?\n\nPatient: Well, high places in general... I don't really think so. I mean, we used to live on the fourteenth floor of this apartment building back in the city. It was a lovely place, right near the park. You could look out the window and see all the trees changing colors in the fall. We had this little balcony, too. My wife would grow tomatoes out there in these big terracotta pots. She had quite the green thumb, she did. She'd talk to the plants, you know, said it helped them grow. I thought it was silly, but those tomatoes were the sweetest things you ever tasted. We'd slice them up thick and put them on a sandwich with a little mayonnaise and some fresh black pepper. Oh, and the bread... there was this Italian bakery down on the corner that made the best sourdough. The crust was so thick and chewy, you really had to work at it. The baker, Tony, he'd always give me an extra loaf if I came in right before closing. He was a good man, Tony. Had a son who played baseball for the minor leagues...\n\nBut, no, the fourteenth floor never bothered me. Looking out over the balcony, being up high like that... it was fine. It really is just... just the bridges. I don't know why, but it's just the bridges.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Well, heights... you know, I think I just answered that, didn't I? We were just talking about the apartment, the one with the balcony on the fourteenth floor. Tony's bakery... I wonder if Tony's son ever made it to the majors. He was a pitcher, a southpaw. They say left-handed pitchers have a real advantage because the ball comes at the batter from a different angle. I used to play a little baseball myself when I was a boy. Not pitching, though. I played second base. I wasn't very fast, but I had a good eye for the ball. We'd play in the empty lot behind Mr. Henderson's hardware store until the streetlights came on. Mr. Henderson was a grumpy old sort, always yelling at us if the ball went into his yard. But his wife, she was a sweetheart. She'd bring out a pitcher of lemonade on hot days. It was real lemonade, too, made with fresh lemons and lots of sugar, not that powdered stuff...

But heights, you were asking again? No, I really don't think it's heights. Like I said, the fourteenth floor was fine. It's just... the bridges. It's always just the bridges.

What brings me in is that the moment a bridge crossing becomes unavoidable, my heart just starts pounding out of my chest. My hands go completely damp on the steering wheel, or the armrest if my daughter is driving. I feel this terrible, overwhelming rush of fear before I'm even on the bridge. It's awful. You know, my daughter, she drives a Subaru now. Used to have a Honda, but she traded it in last year. Got a good deal on it, too, the dealership over on Route 9. The salesman was a nice young man, reminded me of my nephew, Thomas. Thomas just graduated with his master's in engineering, if you can believe it. We had a lovely dinner for him at that Italian place downtown, the one with the good garlic bread... but anyway, yes, the bridges. It's just a terrible feeling.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bridges that trigger this fear, or are there other situations that cause you a similar level of anxiety?\n\nPatient: No, it's... it's really just the bridges. I don't have this kind of trouble anywhere else. I mean, I go all over town just fine. Like the other day, I went down to the hardware store on Main Street to get some of those little felt pads for the bottom of my dining room chairs. You know, the hardwood floors get scratched so easily if you aren't careful. We had them refinished back in... oh, it must have been 1998. The fellow who did it, his name was Arthur. Or maybe Artie? He had this beautiful old golden retriever that used to just sit in the back of his truck while he worked. I've always loved dogs. But at my age—I'm 87 now—it's just too much work to walk a dog in the winter. The ice on the sidewalks is terrible around my neighborhood, the city never salts them properly, and you can slip before you even know what happened. But, uh... what was it you were asking me? Oh, right, other situations. No, no, just the bridges. Nothing else makes my heart pound like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself going out of your way to avoid bridges when you can?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. For more than two years now, I’ve been planning every single one of my errands and visits just to avoid them. I’ve completely given up my trips to the San Juan islands, and my theater nights down in Seattle. Oh, the theater in Seattle used to be just marvelous. My late wife and I used to go see the musicals all the time. She adored Rodgers and Hammerstein. We saw *South Pacific* there, oh, it must have been the late 70s? The costumes were so vibrant. I think I still have the playbill in a box up in the attic. It gets terribly dusty up there, though. I really ought to hire the neighbor boy, Billy, to clean it out for me. He mows my lawn on Tuesdays. Good kid, saving up for college. He wants to study agriculture, I think, maybe over at Washington State... but anyway, what was I saying? \n\nOh, right, avoiding the bridges. Yes, I just don't go to Seattle anymore. And if I absolutely *must* cross one, well, it's a whole production. I have to study the route ahead of time, and the timing has to be just so to avoid traffic. Traffic is dreadful lately anyway, people are always looking at their phones. Just last week I saw a man eating a bowl of soup while driving! Can you imagine? Soup! Looked like minestrone. My mother made a wonderful minestrone, she used to put a parmesan rind in the broth to give it flavor. That's the secret, you know. \n\nBut yes, if I have to cross a bridge, I need someone in the car with me, usually my daughter, and I just sit there completely frightened the whole way across.\n\nDoctor: Are there other activities or things you've stopped doing because of your fear of bridges?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I have. I've had to give up a good deal, actually. Like I mentioned, the trips to the islands and the theater outings are completely gone. But I also used to volunteer at the historical society on Thursdays, sorting through the old photographs. Fascinating stuff, really. You'd see pictures of the town from back in the 1920s, when Main Street was still dirt. There was this one photo of the old general store, the one that burned down in '45. My uncle Henry used to buy his chewing tobacco there. He chewed Red Man, always had a tin in his breast pocket. He was a character, Henry was. Used to play the fiddle at the barn dances out in the valley. They don't have barn dances like that anymore, do they? Now it's all... well, I don't even know what the kids listen to these days. Electronic noises, mostly. \n\nBut anyway, yes, I had to stop the volunteering because the quickest way there was over the suspension bridge, and I just couldn't stomach it anymore. So my days have become quite a bit smaller, I suppose you could say. I just stay closer to home. And I'm relying on my daughter and others for plans I used to handle all by myself. It's frustrating, being 87 and feeling like you can't even drive yourself to sort some old pictures. But that bridge... it's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of bridges affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say it's ruined any relationships, no. My daughter is very patient with me, bless her heart. She takes me where I need to go if it involves a bridge. She’s always been a helpful girl. I remember when she was in the Girl Scouts—oh, she must have been about ten or eleven—she sold the most cookies in her entire troop. We had boxes of Thin Mints stacked all the way up to the ceiling in the garage! I used to sneak a sleeve of them into the freezer. Have you ever had a frozen Thin Mint? Oh, they are just spectacular with a cup of black coffee in the afternoon. I still use the percolator my wife bought us in 1968. Makes a much better cup than those little plastic pods everyone uses now. The coffee just tastes... I don't know, richer, somehow. But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, my friends and family. No, they understand, mostly. I mean, I don't see the folks from the historical society as much, but my daughter and the neighbors, they're fine about it. They just know I don't do bridges anymore.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the impact of your fear of bridges on your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's... it's just made my world a bit smaller, you know? I feel a bit more confined. Not that I don't like my home, I love my home. My wife and I bought this house in 1962, right after the Cuban Missile Crisis. I remember we were so worried about the state of the world, but we wanted a place with a nice yard. And we got one! I planted a dogwood tree in the front that very first spring. It blooms these beautiful white flowers every April. The petals look like little crosses, if you look closely. The neighbor's cat, Whiskers, likes to sleep under it in the summer. He’s an orange tabby, quite fat, really. I think Mrs. Higgins feeds him too much tuna. I told her, \"Mildred, you're going to give that cat gout,\" but she just laughed. She makes a lovely rhubarb pie, though. Brings me a slice every Sunday. Rhubarb is tricky to grow, you know, you have to make sure the soil is just right... \n\nBut, well, I'm not really sure how else to describe the impact, honestly. It's just a nuisance, having to avoid the bridges, but I manage well enough here at the house.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically about the bridge itself, or does the thought of traveling to places that might require crossing a bridge also cause you anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, it's really the bridge itself, I suppose. I mean, I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without a problem. Logically, I know they're built to hold all that weight. They use reinforced steel and concrete, you know. My brother-in-law, Arthur, he used to work in construction. Not bridges, mostly commercial buildings downtown. He helped pour the foundation for the old bank building on 4th Street. He always said concrete is only as good as the rebar you put in it. Arthur was a smart man, could fix any engine you put in front of him. He had this old Ford pickup, a '54, I think. Painted it cherry red himself in his driveway. The smell of that paint lingered for weeks. His wife, my sister Margaret, she wasn't too happy about that. She was very particular about smells, Margaret was. Couldn't stand the smell of garlic, either. Made Italian dinners very difficult at her house... \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, the bridges. Even knowing they're safe, when I just see a high span on a map, or if I spot one ahead through the windshield... my heart just drops. It feels to me as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away right under us. So, yes, just the thought of the bridge itself is what really gets me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges worse when the bridge is particularly high or over water?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the height is the worst part. If it's one of those little stone bridges over a creek, I don't mind so much. But those big ones, the ones that go way up into the sky... oh, I just can't. And over water, yes, that's terrible too. Especially the ocean or a big river. The Mississippi River is quite a sight, isn't it? My wife and I took a riverboat cruise down the Mississippi back in '85. Or was it '86? No, it must have been '85 because that was the year the Chicago Bears won the Super Bowl. What a team they had that year. Walter Payton, they called him \"Sweetness.\" He ran like a gazelle, just beautiful to watch. I used to play football in high school, you know. I was a halfback. We didn't have the fancy helmets they have now, just leather caps. I broke my nose twice! My mother was furious. She said I was going to ruin my handsome face. She was a very strict woman, my mother. She used to make us eat liver and onions every Tuesday... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the high bridges and the ones over the water are definitely the ones that terrify me the most. I just can't go near them.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges the same regardless of whether you are driving, walking, or a passenger?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I haven't really noticed if it makes a difference. To be honest, I haven't tried walking across one in, oh, it must be decades. I remember walking across the Golden Gate Bridge once, a long time ago. My wife and I went to San Francisco for our tenth anniversary. We took one of those cable cars, you know, the ones that go up and down the steep hills. The conductor was a fellow named Giuseppe, had a mustache you wouldn't believe, curled right up at the ends. He told us he used to be a baker in North Beach. Said the secret to a good sourdough is the starter, you have to feed it every day like a pet. We bought a loaf from a bakery down by the wharf, ate it right out of the paper bag while we watched the sea lions. They make such a racket, those sea lions! Barking and carrying on, fighting over space on the wooden docks. Reminded me of my nephews, Tommy and Billy, always wrestling in the living room... \n\nBut, no, I don't really know if it's worse driving or being a passenger. I just know I don't want to be on one at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges is partly because of how high they are, or is it something else about the bridges themselves that scares you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really don't know for sure. I mean, we talked about the height earlier, and yes, that's dreadful. But is it just the height? Or something else? I couldn't say exactly. It's just the whole... the whole structure of it, I suppose. \n\nYou know, speaking of structures, my grandfather used to build these incredible birdhouses. Not just little boxes, mind you. They were like miniature mansions. He built one that looked exactly like a Victorian home, complete with tiny little shingles he cut from cedar scraps. He'd spend hours in his workshop out back. It smelled like sawdust and pipe tobacco in there. He smoked Cherry Blend, came in a red and white pouch. My grandmother hated it, said it made the curtains smell. She was always washing the curtains, it seemed. Had this old wringer washer on the back porch. You had to be so careful feeding the clothes through those rollers, or you'd pinch your fingers something awful. My cousin Sally got her hair caught in one once! Oh, what a commotion that was. We had to cut a big chunk of her hair right off. She cried for three days straight, wouldn't even come out of her room for Sunday dinner. And my grandmother made the best pot roast for Sunday dinner, with carrots and potatoes roasted right in the juices...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, what was it you were asking? If it's the height or something else? I really just don't know. It's just the bridges themselves, the whole idea of them, that I can't stand.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar kind of fear when you are in other high places, like tall buildings or mountains?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not the height itself, really. I mean, I can stand in a tall building just fine. I remember when they finished the Sears Tower in Chicago, oh, what a marvel that was. I took my son up to the observation deck when he was just a little boy. We stood right against the glass and looked out over the whole city. You could see all the way to Indiana on a clear day. He had this little toy airplane he carried everywhere, a die-cast metal P-51 Mustang. He kept zooming it against the window, pretending he was flying over the buildings. He wanted to be a pilot for the longest time, right up until he realized he needed glasses. Poor kid was devastated. We ended up getting him these thick, black-rimmed glasses, just like Buddy Holly used to wear. You remember Buddy Holly? \"Peggy Sue,\" \"That'll Be the Day\"... such a tragedy, that plane crash. The day the music died, they called it. Don McLean wrote a whole song about it, a very long song. I heard it on the radio just the other day while I was trying to tune in the baseball game...\n\nBut, no, buildings or mountains don't bother me. It's just the bridges. I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without a problem. It's perfectly safe, logically. But when I see a high span on the map, or if I see one looming ahead through the windshield... oh, it just feels to me as if the whole thing is waiting to drop away right out from under us. Just collapse completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience anxiety or fear when in other high places, such as standing near a cliff or at the top of a ladder?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I don't really know for sure. Ladders, you say? I haven't been up on a ladder in years. My son comes over to clean the gutters now. He's got one of those fancy aluminum extension ladders, very lightweight. Not like the old wooden one I used to have. That thing weighed a ton. I bought it at a hardware store downtown, back when downtown was still bustling. There was a soda fountain right next door, Woolworth's, I think it was. They made the best cherry phosphates. You don't see cherry phosphates anymore. It's all these energy drinks now, in those brightly colored cans. My grandson drinks them like water. I tell him all that sugar and caffeine can't be good for his heart, but you know teenagers. They think they're invincible. He's playing football this year, defensive tackle. He's a big boy, gets it from his mother's side... \n\nBut, what were you asking? Cliffs and ladders? I don't think I've really noticed if they bother me. It's just the bridges that get me so worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when standing on a balcony of a high-rise building?\n\nPatient: Balconies? Oh, I haven't been out on a balcony in... well, I couldn't tell you the last time. My wife and I, we stayed in a lovely hotel in Miami once, must have been back in the late seventies. Or was it the early eighties? No, it was '79, because that was the year they had that terrible winter up north, snowed all through February. We had a room right on the ocean, with a little wrought-iron balcony. Beautiful scrolling work on it, painted white. We'd sit out there in the mornings and watch the seagulls. They were so bold, those gulls. One of them swooped right down and stole a piece of toast right off my plate! Can you believe it? Just snatched it and flew off. We laughed about that for years. We used to have toast with orange marmalade. You just don't find good marmalade these days, not like they used to make. Now it's all just sugar jelly with a few peels thrown in. My mother used to make her own preserves, apricot and plum. She'd spend the whole end of summer canning in the kitchen. It was so hot in there, but she'd just tie an apron on and get to work... \n\nBut, I'm sorry, you were asking about standing on balconies? I really haven't had the occasion to try it lately, so I just don't know if it would bother me. It's really only those bridges that give me that dreadful feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar kind of fear when you're in other situations that might make you feel like you're in a high place, but not necessarily on a bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really don't think so. Other high places, you mean? Like an airplane or something? We flew out to California back in '92 for my niece's wedding. She married a lovely boy, an architect. They designed their own house out there in Pasadena, beautiful place with these big exposed wooden beams. Cedar, I think they were. Smelled wonderful when you walked in. They served prime rib at the reception, but it was a bit too rare for my taste. I always liked my meat cooked through. My father was a butcher, you know. Had his own shop on 4th Street. He could carve a roast like nobody's business. Used this big, heavy cleaver with a wooden handle that was worn smooth from years of use. He'd sharpen it every morning on a whetstone. The sound of that stone scraping against the steel, shhhk, shhhk, shhhk... it's funny the things you remember. He used to slip me a piece of bologna when I'd come in after school... \n\nBut, no, I don't think I have that same feeling in other high places. It really is just the bridges. Just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when on a bridge, compared to when you're on another type of elevated structure, like an overpass?\n\nPatient: Well, an overpass... I suppose I haven't really thought about the difference. An overpass is just a road over another road, isn't it? We used to drive over the one on I-95 all the time when we'd go visit my sister in Connecticut. She lived in this charming little colonial house in Milford. Had a big oak tree in the front yard that must have been a hundred years old. In the autumn, the leaves would turn this brilliant shade of orange, and we'd spend all afternoon raking them into massive piles. The kids would just dive right into them, laughing and getting leaves all in their hair. My sister had a golden retriever back then, Barnaby. Oh, he was a good dog. He'd jump in the leaves right along with the kids. He had this funny habit of carrying a tennis ball around in his mouth everywhere he went. Even when he was sleeping, he'd have that ball tucked right under his chin. Poor old Barnaby, he got terrible arthritis in his back legs toward the end... \n\nBut an overpass, you were asking? No, I don't think overpasses bother me. It's just the bridges over water, mostly. Just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges limited to certain types, such as suspension bridges or those with a certain design?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't really know if it's the design. Suspension bridges, you mean? Like the Golden Gate? We saw that when we went to California, too. We didn't drive over it, though, thank goodness. We took a ferry across the bay instead. Oh, the ferry was wonderful! The wind blowing in your hair, the smell of the salt water. They sold these little cups of clam chowder on board. It wasn't as good as New England clam chowder, of course. My wife made the best chowder. She'd use fresh clams we'd dig up ourselves when we vacationed in Cape Cod. You have to soak them in saltwater first, you know, with a little cornmeal, so they spit out the sand. If you don't do that, your soup is all gritty. Nobody likes gritty soup. She'd serve it with those little oyster crackers... the ones shaped like hexagons. I used to buy them in big tins at the grocer... \n\nBut, I'm sorry, you were asking about the types of bridges? I really couldn't tell you if it's the suspension ones or the arches or what. I just know that when I see a bridge, especially a big one over the water, my heart just starts pounding. I don't think I've ever really paid attention to the design, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges might be connected to a fear of being in high places in general?\n\nPatient: Well, high places in general... I don't really think so. I mean, we used to live on the fourteenth floor of this apartment building back in the city. It was a lovely place, right near the park. You could look out the window and see all the trees changing colors in the fall. We had this little balcony, too. My wife would grow tomatoes out there in these big terracotta pots. She had quite the green thumb, she did. She'd talk to the plants, you know, said it helped them grow. I thought it was silly, but those tomatoes were the sweetest things you ever tasted. We'd slice them up thick and put them on a sandwich with a little mayonnaise and some fresh black pepper. Oh, and the bread... there was this Italian bakery down on the corner that made the best sourdough. The crust was so thick and chewy, you really had to work at it. The baker, Tony, he'd always give me an extra loaf if I came in right before closing. He was a good man, Tony. Had a son who played baseball for the minor leagues...\n\nBut, no, the fourteenth floor never bothered me. Looking out over the balcony, being up high like that... it was fine. It really is just... just the bridges. I don't know why, but it's just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges is related to a fear of heights or being in high places?\n\nPatient: Well, heights... you know, I think I just answered that, didn't I? We were just talking about the apartment, the one with the balcony on the fourteenth floor. Tony's bakery... I wonder if Tony's son ever made it to the majors. He was a pitcher, a southpaw. They say left-handed pitchers have a real advantage because the ball comes at the batter from a different angle. I used to play a little baseball myself when I was a boy. Not pitching, though. I played second base. I wasn't very fast, but I had a good eye for the ball. We'd play in the empty lot behind Mr. Henderson's hardware store until the streetlights came on. Mr. Henderson was a grumpy old sort, always yelling at us if the ball went into his yard. But his wife, she was a sweetheart. She'd bring out a pitcher of lemonade on hot days. It was real lemonade, too, made with fresh lemons and lots of sugar, not that powdered stuff...\n\nBut heights, you were asking again? No, I really don't think it's heights. Like I said, the fourteenth floor was fine. It's just... the bridges. It's always just the bridges.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

She makes a lovely rhubarb pie, though. Brings me a slice every Sunday. Rhubarb is tricky to grow, you know, you have to make sure the soil is just right... \n\nBut, well, I'm not really sure how else to describe the impact, honestly. It's just a nuisance, having to avoid the bridges, but I manage well enough here at the house.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear specifically about the bridge itself, or does the thought of traveling to places that might require crossing a bridge also cause you anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, it's really the bridge itself, I suppose. I mean, I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without a problem. Logically, I know they're built to hold all that weight. They use reinforced steel and concrete, you know. My brother-in-law, Arthur, he used to work in construction. Not bridges, mostly commercial buildings downtown. He helped pour the foundation for the old bank building on 4th Street. He always said concrete is only as good as the rebar you put in it. Arthur was a smart man, could fix any engine you put in front of him. He had this old Ford pickup, a '54, I think. Painted it cherry red himself in his driveway. The smell of that paint lingered for weeks. His wife, my sister Margaret, she wasn't too happy about that. She was very particular about smells, Margaret was. Couldn't stand the smell of garlic, either. Made Italian dinners very difficult at her house... \n\nBut, oh, what was I saying? Right, the bridges. Even knowing they're safe, when I just see a high span on a map, or if I spot one ahead through the windshield... my heart just drops. It feels to me as if the whole thing is just waiting to drop away right under us. So, yes, just the thought of the bridge itself is what really gets me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges worse when the bridge is particularly high or over water?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose the height is the worst part. If it's one of those little stone bridges over a creek, I don't mind so much. But those big ones, the ones that go way up into the sky... oh, I just can't. And over water, yes, that's terrible too. Especially the ocean or a big river. The Mississippi River is quite a sight, isn't it? My wife and I took a riverboat cruise down the Mississippi back in '85. Or was it '86? No, it must have been '85 because that was the year the Chicago Bears won the Super Bowl. What a team they had that year. Walter Payton, they called him \"Sweetness.\" He ran like a gazelle, just beautiful to watch. I used to play football in high school, you know. I was a halfback. We didn't have the fancy helmets they have now, just leather caps. I broke my nose twice! My mother was furious. She said I was going to ruin my handsome face. She was a very strict woman, my mother. She used to make us eat liver and onions every Tuesday... \n\nBut anyway, yes, the high bridges and the ones over the water are definitely the ones that terrify me the most. I just can't go near them.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges the same regardless of whether you are driving, walking, or a passenger?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I haven't really noticed if it makes a difference. To be honest, I haven't tried walking across one in, oh, it must be decades. I remember walking across the Golden Gate Bridge once, a long time ago. My wife and I went to San Francisco for our tenth anniversary. We took one of those cable cars, you know, the ones that go up and down the steep hills. The conductor was a fellow named Giuseppe, had a mustache you wouldn't believe, curled right up at the ends. He told us he used to be a baker in North Beach. Said the secret to a good sourdough is the starter, you have to feed it every day like a pet. We bought a loaf from a bakery down by the wharf, ate it right out of the paper bag while we watched the sea lions. They make such a racket, those sea lions! Barking and carrying on, fighting over space on the wooden docks. Reminded me of my nephews, Tommy and Billy, always wrestling in the living room... \n\nBut, no, I don't really know if it's worse driving or being a passenger. I just know I don't want to be on one at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges is partly because of how high they are, or is it something else about the bridges themselves that scares you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really don't know for sure. I mean, we talked about the height earlier, and yes, that's dreadful. But is it just the height? Or something else? I couldn't say exactly. It's just the whole... the whole structure of it, I suppose. \n\nYou know, speaking of structures, my grandfather used to build these incredible birdhouses. Not just little boxes, mind you. They were like miniature mansions. He built one that looked exactly like a Victorian home, complete with tiny little shingles he cut from cedar scraps. He'd spend hours in his workshop out back. It smelled like sawdust and pipe tobacco in there. He smoked Cherry Blend, came in a red and white pouch. My grandmother hated it, said it made the curtains smell. She was always washing the curtains, it seemed. Had this old wringer washer on the back porch. You had to be so careful feeding the clothes through those rollers, or you'd pinch your fingers something awful. My cousin Sally got her hair caught in one once! Oh, what a commotion that was. We had to cut a big chunk of her hair right off. She cried for three days straight, wouldn't even come out of her room for Sunday dinner. And my grandmother made the best pot roast for Sunday dinner, with carrots and potatoes roasted right in the juices...\n\nBut, I'm sorry, what was it you were asking? If it's the height or something else? I really just don't know. It's just the bridges themselves, the whole idea of them, that I can't stand.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar kind of fear when you are in other high places, like tall buildings or mountains?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's not the height itself, really. I mean, I can stand in a tall building just fine. I remember when they finished the Sears Tower in Chicago, oh, what a marvel that was. I took my son up to the observation deck when he was just a little boy. We stood right against the glass and looked out over the whole city. You could see all the way to Indiana on a clear day. He had this little toy airplane he carried everywhere, a die-cast metal P-51 Mustang. He kept zooming it against the window, pretending he was flying over the buildings. He wanted to be a pilot for the longest time, right up until he realized he needed glasses. Poor kid was devastated. We ended up getting him these thick, black-rimmed glasses, just like Buddy Holly used to wear. You remember Buddy Holly? \"Peggy Sue,\" \"That'll Be the Day\"... such a tragedy, that plane crash. The day the music died, they called it. Don McLean wrote a whole song about it, a very long song. I heard it on the radio just the other day while I was trying to tune in the baseball game...\n\nBut, no, buildings or mountains don't bother me. It's just the bridges. I know perfectly well that thousands of cars cross those bridges every day without a problem. It's perfectly safe, logically. But when I see a high span on the map, or if I see one looming ahead through the windshield... oh, it just feels to me as if the whole thing is waiting to drop away right out from under us. Just collapse completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience anxiety or fear when in other high places, such as standing near a cliff or at the top of a ladder?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I don't really know for sure. Ladders, you say? I haven't been up on a ladder in years. My son comes over to clean the gutters now. He's got one of those fancy aluminum extension ladders, very lightweight. Not like the old wooden one I used to have. That thing weighed a ton. I bought it at a hardware store downtown, back when downtown was still bustling. There was a soda fountain right next door, Woolworth's, I think it was. They made the best cherry phosphates. You don't see cherry phosphates anymore. It's all these energy drinks now, in those brightly colored cans. My grandson drinks them like water. I tell him all that sugar and caffeine can't be good for his heart, but you know teenagers. They think they're invincible. He's playing football this year, defensive tackle. He's a big boy, gets it from his mother's side... \n\nBut, what were you asking? Cliffs and ladders? I don't think I've really noticed if they bother me. It's just the bridges that get me so worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when standing on a balcony of a high-rise building?\n\nPatient: Balconies? Oh, I haven't been out on a balcony in... well, I couldn't tell you the last time. My wife and I, we stayed in a lovely hotel in Miami once, must have been back in the late seventies. Or was it the early eighties? No, it was '79, because that was the year they had that terrible winter up north, snowed all through February. We had a room right on the ocean, with a little wrought-iron balcony. Beautiful scrolling work on it, painted white. We'd sit out there in the mornings and watch the seagulls. They were so bold, those gulls. One of them swooped right down and stole a piece of toast right off my plate! Can you believe it? Just snatched it and flew off. We laughed about that for years. We used to have toast with orange marmalade. You just don't find good marmalade these days, not like they used to make. Now it's all just sugar jelly with a few peels thrown in. My mother used to make her own preserves, apricot and plum. She'd spend the whole end of summer canning in the kitchen. It was so hot in there, but she'd just tie an apron on and get to work... \n\nBut, I'm sorry, you were asking about standing on balconies? I really haven't had the occasion to try it lately, so I just don't know if it would bother me. It's really only those bridges that give me that dreadful feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience a similar kind of fear when you're in other situations that might make you feel like you're in a high place, but not necessarily on a bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really don't think so. Other high places, you mean? Like an airplane or something? We flew out to California back in '92 for my niece's wedding. She married a lovely boy, an architect. They designed their own house out there in Pasadena, beautiful place with these big exposed wooden beams. Cedar, I think they were. Smelled wonderful when you walked in. They served prime rib at the reception, but it was a bit too rare for my taste. I always liked my meat cooked through. My father was a butcher, you know. Had his own shop on 4th Street. He could carve a roast like nobody's business. Used this big, heavy cleaver with a wooden handle that was worn smooth from years of use. He'd sharpen it every morning on a whetstone. The sound of that stone scraping against the steel, shhhk, shhhk, shhhk... it's funny the things you remember. He used to slip me a piece of bologna when I'd come in after school... \n\nBut, no, I don't think I have that same feeling in other high places. It really is just the bridges. Just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or fearful when on a bridge, compared to when you're on another type of elevated structure, like an overpass?\n\nPatient: Well, an overpass... I suppose I haven't really thought about the difference. An overpass is just a road over another road, isn't it? We used to drive over the one on I-95 all the time when we'd go visit my sister in Connecticut. She lived in this charming little colonial house in Milford. Had a big oak tree in the front yard that must have been a hundred years old. In the autumn, the leaves would turn this brilliant shade of orange, and we'd spend all afternoon raking them into massive piles. The kids would just dive right into them, laughing and getting leaves all in their hair. My sister had a golden retriever back then, Barnaby. Oh, he was a good dog. He'd jump in the leaves right along with the kids. He had this funny habit of carrying a tennis ball around in his mouth everywhere he went. Even when he was sleeping, he'd have that ball tucked right under his chin. Poor old Barnaby, he got terrible arthritis in his back legs toward the end... \n\nBut an overpass, you were asking? No, I don't think overpasses bother me. It's just the bridges over water, mostly. Just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges limited to certain types, such as suspension bridges or those with a certain design?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't really know if it's the design. Suspension bridges, you mean? Like the Golden Gate? We saw that when we went to California, too. We didn't drive over it, though, thank goodness. We took a ferry across the bay instead. Oh, the ferry was wonderful! The wind blowing in your hair, the smell of the salt water. They sold these little cups of clam chowder on board. It wasn't as good as New England clam chowder, of course. My wife made the best chowder. She'd use fresh clams we'd dig up ourselves when we vacationed in Cape Cod. You have to soak them in saltwater first, you know, with a little cornmeal, so they spit out the sand. If you don't do that, your soup is all gritty. Nobody likes gritty soup. She'd serve it with those little oyster crackers... the ones shaped like hexagons. I used to buy them in big tins at the grocer... \n\nBut, I'm sorry, you were asking about the types of bridges? I really couldn't tell you if it's the suspension ones or the arches or what. I just know that when I see a bridge, especially a big one over the water, my heart just starts pounding. I don't think I've ever really paid attention to the design, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges might be connected to a fear of being in high places in general?\n\nPatient: Well, high places in general... I don't really think so. I mean, we used to live on the fourteenth floor of this apartment building back in the city. It was a lovely place, right near the park. You could look out the window and see all the trees changing colors in the fall. We had this little balcony, too. My wife would grow tomatoes out there in these big terracotta pots. She had quite the green thumb, she did. She'd talk to the plants, you know, said it helped them grow. I thought it was silly, but those tomatoes were the sweetest things you ever tasted. We'd slice them up thick and put them on a sandwich with a little mayonnaise and some fresh black pepper. Oh, and the bread... there was this Italian bakery down on the corner that made the best sourdough. The crust was so thick and chewy, you really had to work at it. The baker, Tony, he'd always give me an extra loaf if I came in right before closing. He was a good man, Tony. Had a son who played baseball for the minor leagues...\n\nBut, no, the fourteenth floor never bothered me. Looking out over the balcony, being up high like that... it was fine. It really is just... just the bridges. I don't know why, but it's just the bridges.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of bridges is related to a fear of heights or being in high places?\n\nPatient: Well, heights... you know, I think I just answered that, didn't I? We were just talking about the apartment, the one with the balcony on the fourteenth floor. Tony's bakery... I wonder if Tony's son ever made it to the majors. He was a pitcher, a southpaw. They say left-handed pitchers have a real advantage because the ball comes at the batter from a different angle. I used to play a little baseball myself when I was a boy. Not pitching, though. I played second base. I wasn't very fast, but I had a good eye for the ball. We'd play in the empty lot behind Mr. Henderson's hardware store until the streetlights came on. Mr. Henderson was a grumpy old sort, always yelling at us if the ball went into his yard. But his wife, she was a sweetheart. She'd bring out a pitcher of lemonade on hot days. It was real lemonade, too, made with fresh lemons and lots of sugar, not that powdered stuff...\n\nBut heights, you were asking again? No, I really don't think it's heights. Like I said, the fourteenth floor was fine. It's just... the bridges. It's always just the bridges.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Water below... well, I couldn't really say if it's the water that makes it worse. I mean, water is just water. We used to spend a lot of time on the water, actually. My brother-in-law, Arthur, he had this beautiful old wooden boat. A Chris-Craft, all mahogany. Oh, it was a gorgeous thing, but the upkeep was tremendous. Every single spring we'd be out in his driveway sanding it down and applying fresh marine varnish. The smell of that varnish, it just takes me right back to 1965. We'd take it out on Lake George early in the morning to fish for bass. Arthur swore by nightcrawlers. We'd go out into the yard after a good summer rain with a flashlight—you had to put a piece of red cellophane over the lens, you see, because the bright white light would spook the worms and they'd shoot right back down into their holes. You had to be quick to grab them. My grandson tried it once when he was little, but he didn't like getting his hands muddy. Kids today, they spend all their time on those phones, they don't want to get their hands dirty...

But, I'm sorry, what were we saying? You asked about seeing the water? I really don't know if that changes anything for me. If there's a bridge, whether it's over a river or a highway or a dry ditch... it just absolutely paralyzes me. I haven't really noticed if the water makes the panic any worse. It's all just dreadful, to be honest.
